# RCE Anesthesiology Practice Exam — Set 02 — Keys and Rationales

> Independently reviewed and source-verified. Human specialist review remains pending.

# Paper 1

## S02-Q001

Domain 1 · Assessment and prediction

Which bedside airway-assessment approach most directly estimates the mandibular space available for tongue displacement?

A. Measure the thyromental distance.

B. Repeat Mallampati scoring during pregnancy and labor.

C. Combine Mallampati scoring with other airway assessments.

D. Use the Mallampati score as an indicator of laryngoscopy and intubation difficulty.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Measure the thyromental distance.

The thyromental distance estimates the size of the mandibular space available for tongue displacement.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Estimate the mandibular space available for tongue displacement using the thyromental distance.

**B:** Repeat Mallampati scoring during pregnancy and labor.

Mallampati scores can change during pregnancy and during labor.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Repeat Mallampati scoring during pregnancy and labor.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Estimate the mandibular space available for tongue displacement using the thyromental distance.

**C:** Combine Mallampati scoring with other airway assessments.

Use the Mallampati score together with other airway assessments rather than as a sole predictor of difficult intubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Combine Mallampati scoring with other airway assessments.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Estimate the mandibular space available for tongue displacement using the thyromental distance.

**D:** Use the Mallampati score as an indicator of laryngoscopy and intubation difficulty.

Increasing Mallampati scores are associated with increasing difficulty with laryngoscopy and tracheal intubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use the Mallampati score as an indicator of laryngoscopy and intubation difficulty.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Estimate the mandibular space available for tongue displacement using the thyromental distance.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 764, passage 273626

## S02-Q002

Domain 1 · Assessment and prediction

Which finding–implication pairing directly describes the consequence of limited mouth opening during direct laryngoscopy?

A. A beard — difficulty obtaining a mask seal.

B. Limited mouth opening — difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

C. Mallampati grade — the relationship among mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space.

D. A short thyromental distance — limited neck mobility or retrognathia.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: B**

**A:** A beard — difficulty obtaining a mask seal.

A beard is associated with difficulty obtaining a mask seal.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A beard — difficulty obtaining a mask seal.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize limited mouth opening as a mechanical contributor to difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

**B — correct:** Limited mouth opening — difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

Limited mouth opening can make blade insertion and tongue displacement difficult.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize limited mouth opening as a mechanical contributor to difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

**C:** Mallampati grade — the relationship among mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space.

Mallampati grade describes the relationship among mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Mallampati grade — the relationship among mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize limited mouth opening as a mechanical contributor to difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

**D:** A short thyromental distance — limited neck mobility or retrognathia.

A short thyromental distance is concerning because it reflects limited neck mobility or retrognathia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A short thyromental distance — limited neck mobility or retrognathia.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize limited mouth opening as a mechanical contributor to difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 2302, passage 247157

## S02-Q003

Domain 1 · Preoxygenation and mask ventilation

A severely obese patient cannot be positioned head-up and will undergo supine preoxygenation with CPAP. Which documented benefit best supports using 10 cm H2O rather than no positive pressure?

A. Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and postinduction ventilation to extend nonhypoxemic apnea from 2 to 3 minutes.

B. Use a 25-degree head-up position rather than supine positioning without positive airway pressure to increase time to desaturation.

C. Ten cm H2O of CPAP during supine preoxygenation produced higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis.

D. Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP supine for 3 minutes, which did not change time to desaturation to SpO2 90%.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and postinduction ventilation to extend nonhypoxemic apnea from 2 to 3 minutes.

Combining CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and ventilation after induction increased nonhypoxemic apnea duration from 2 to 3 minutes.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and postinduction ventilation to extend nonhypoxemic apnea from 2 to 3 minutes.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize how supine CPAP during preoxygenation changes post-intubation oxygenation and atelectasis in obesity.

**B:** Use a 25-degree head-up position rather than supine positioning without positive airway pressure to increase time to desaturation.

In an obese patient, preoxygenation in a 25-degree head-up position increases the time to desaturation compared with supine positioning without positive airway pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use a 25-degree head-up position rather than supine positioning without positive airway pressure to increase time to desaturation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize how supine CPAP during preoxygenation changes post-intubation oxygenation and atelectasis in obesity.

**C — correct:** Ten cm H2O of CPAP during supine preoxygenation produced higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis.

Ten cm H2O of CPAP during supine preoxygenation produced a higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize how supine CPAP during preoxygenation changes post-intubation oxygenation and atelectasis in obesity.

**D:** Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP supine for 3 minutes, which did not change time to desaturation to SpO2 90%.

Using 7.5 cm H2O CPAP for 3 minutes while supine did not change the time for obese patients to desaturate to an SpO2 of 90%.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP supine for 3 minutes, which did not change time to desaturation to SpO2 90%.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize how supine CPAP during preoxygenation changes post-intubation oxygenation and atelectasis in obesity.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2136, passage 290554

## S02-Q004

Domain 1 · Preoxygenation and mask ventilation

Only 30 seconds are available before induction, so the team is weighing a rapid breathing regimen against longer tidal-volume preoxygenation. Which statement captures the four-breath tradeoff?

A. Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.

B. Pregnancy-associated reduced functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.

C. Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method.

D. Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection from rapid desaturation.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.

Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Compare abbreviated maximal-breath preoxygenation with standard tidal-volume breathing when rapid desaturation is a concern.

**B:** Pregnancy-associated reduced functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.

Pregnancy-associated reduction in functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Pregnancy-associated reduced functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Compare abbreviated maximal-breath preoxygenation with standard tidal-volume breathing when rapid desaturation is a concern.

**C:** Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method.

Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method described for this setting.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Compare abbreviated maximal-breath preoxygenation with standard tidal-volume breathing when rapid desaturation is a concern.

**D — correct:** Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection from rapid desaturation.

Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection from rapid desaturation.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Compare abbreviated maximal-breath preoxygenation with standard tidal-volume breathing when rapid desaturation is a concern.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 600, passage 272842

## S02-Q005

Domain 1 · Airway devices and intubation

Under the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines Plan-A strategy, a hyperangulated videolaryngoscope provides the view but tube delivery remains difficult. Which adjunct is described for use with that blade?

A. Use a flexible bronchoscope as an adjunct with the hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade.

B. Use direct laryngoscopy rather than videolaryngoscopy as the first-line technique.

C. Use a videolaryngoscope first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.

D. Use an optical stylet as an additional alternative device for intubation.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Use a flexible bronchoscope as an adjunct with the hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade.

The guideline places the flexible bronchoscope in an adjunct role ('A stylet, bougie, or flexible bronchoscope should be used with a hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade'), used together with a videolaryngoscope rather than named as the first-line device itself.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the DAS 2025 relationship between videolaryngoscopy and adjunctive flexible bronchoscope use.

**B:** Use direct laryngoscopy rather than videolaryngoscopy as the first-line technique.

The guideline names videolaryngoscopy, not direct laryngoscopy, as the technique to be used first line; the recommending sentence explicitly contrasts videolaryngoscopy 'compared with direct laryngoscopy.' Direct laryngoscopy is not the device the guideline designates first line.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use direct laryngoscopy rather than videolaryngoscopy as the first-line technique.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the DAS 2025 relationship between videolaryngoscopy and adjunctive flexible bronchoscope use.

**C:** Use a videolaryngoscope first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.

The Difficult Airway Society 2025 guidelines state that a videolaryngoscope should be used first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use a videolaryngoscope first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the DAS 2025 relationship between videolaryngoscopy and adjunctive flexible bronchoscope use.

**D:** Use an optical stylet as an additional alternative device for intubation.

The guideline describes the optical stylet as an additional device that intubation 'can also be achieved with,' listed after and distinct from the first-line videolaryngoscope recommendation, not as the designated first-line device.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use an optical stylet as an additional alternative device for intubation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the DAS 2025 relationship between videolaryngoscopy and adjunctive flexible bronchoscope use.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 607, passage 185312
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 6, passage 317270
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 7, passage 317271
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 8, passage 317276

## S02-Q011

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

A patient's cardiac output falls after induction. Which framework most completely identifies the direct determinants that should be assessed?

A. The relation of preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.

B. Heart rate, preload, myocardial contractility, and afterload.

C. The linear end-systolic pressure-volume relationship.

D. The effect of preload alone on stroke volume.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** The relation of preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.

The cardiac function curve relates preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The relation of preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the four principal determinants of cardiac output.

**B — correct:** Heart rate, preload, myocardial contractility, and afterload.

Heart rate, preload, myocardial contractility, and afterload are the four factors determining cardiac output.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Identify the four principal determinants of cardiac output.

**C:** The linear end-systolic pressure-volume relationship.

The end-systolic pressure-volume relationship is represented as a straight line in the pressure-volume description.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The linear end-systolic pressure-volume relationship.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the four principal determinants of cardiac output.

**D:** The effect of preload alone on stroke volume.

Increasing preload increases stroke volume on the cardiac function curve.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The effect of preload alone on stroke volume.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the four principal determinants of cardiac output.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 348, passage 282112

## S02-Q012

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

When estimating left-ventricular coronary perfusion, why is aortic diastolic rather than systolic pressure used as the upstream pressure term?

A. Because systolic contraction reduces intraventricular pressure and coronary resistance.

B. Because higher aortic pressure lowers afterload and wall tension.

C. Because 70% to 80% of coronary arterial flow occurs during diastole.

D. Because LVEDP replaces aortic pressure during diastole.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Because systolic contraction reduces intraventricular pressure and coronary resistance.

Systolic contraction increases intraventricular pressure and coronary vascular resistance, impeding myocardial perfusion.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Because systolic contraction reduces intraventricular pressure and coronary resistance.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the aortic diastolic pressure term in the left-ventricular coronary-perfusion calculation.

**B:** Because higher aortic pressure lowers afterload and wall tension.

A rise in blood pressure increases coronary flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and myocardial oxygen demand.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Because higher aortic pressure lowers afterload and wall tension.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the aortic diastolic pressure term in the left-ventricular coronary-perfusion calculation.

**C — correct:** Because 70% to 80% of coronary arterial flow occurs during diastole.

Aortic diastolic pressure is physiologically relevant because 70% to 80% of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Identify the aortic diastolic pressure term in the left-ventricular coronary-perfusion calculation.

**D:** Because LVEDP replaces aortic pressure during diastole.

Elevated LVEDP impedes subendocardial blood flow and is subtracted from aortic diastolic pressure when calculating left-ventricular coronary perfusion pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Because LVEDP replaces aortic pressure during diastole.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the aortic diastolic pressure term in the left-ventricular coronary-perfusion calculation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1936, passage 289609
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2370, passage 291742
- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 3287, passage 248886
- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 967, passage 244884

## S02-Q013

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

Across a tissue bed, the pressure difference doubles while vascular resistance remains constant. What happens to blood flow?

A. It is unchanged because only viscosity and vessel length determine resistance.

B. It cannot be determined without mean arterial pressure, right atrial pressure, and cardiac output.

C. It increases sixteenfold because flow follows the fourth power of vessel radius.

D. It doubles because flow is proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: D**

**A:** It is unchanged because only viscosity and vessel length determine resistance.

Resistance is directly proportional to blood viscosity and vessel length.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “It is unchanged because only viscosity and vessel length determine resistance.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate tissue blood flow to pressure difference and vascular resistance.

**B:** It cannot be determined without mean arterial pressure, right atrial pressure, and cardiac output.

Systemic vascular resistance is calculated as mean arterial pressure minus right atrial pressure, divided by cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “It cannot be determined without mean arterial pressure, right atrial pressure, and cardiac output.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate tissue blood flow to pressure difference and vascular resistance.

**C:** It increases sixteenfold because flow follows the fourth power of vessel radius.

Resistance is inversely proportional to the fourth power of vessel radius.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “It increases sixteenfold because flow follows the fourth power of vessel radius.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate tissue blood flow to pressure difference and vascular resistance.

**D — correct:** It doubles because flow is proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance.

Tissue blood flow is directly proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Relate tissue blood flow to pressure difference and vascular resistance.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 1184, passage 313797

## S02-Q014

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

A patient with coronary disease becomes markedly tachycardic and develops ischemic changes despite preserved mean arterial pressure. Which physiological fact best explains the loss of coronary oxygen supply?

A. Seventy to eighty percent of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

B. LV coronary perfusion pressure equals aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.

C. Higher blood pressure increases coronary flow while also increasing afterload and oxygen demand.

D. An elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impedes subendocardial blood flow.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Seventy to eighty percent of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

Seventy to eighty percent of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the predominance of coronary arterial flow during diastole.

**B:** LV coronary perfusion pressure equals aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.

Left-ventricular coronary perfusion pressure is aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “LV coronary perfusion pressure equals aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the predominance of coronary arterial flow during diastole.

**C:** Higher blood pressure increases coronary flow while also increasing afterload and oxygen demand.

A rise in blood pressure increases coronary blood flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Higher blood pressure increases coronary flow while also increasing afterload and oxygen demand.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the predominance of coronary arterial flow during diastole.

**D:** An elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impedes subendocardial blood flow.

Elevations in LV end-diastolic pressure impede subendocardial blood flow.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impedes subendocardial blood flow.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the predominance of coronary arterial flow during diastole.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1936, passage 289609

## S02-Q015

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

Under otherwise unchanged conditions, a patient's horizontal ST depression in V5 progresses from 1 mm to 3 mm. Which interpretation is best supported?

A. Both tracings only cross a binary ischemia threshold, so the magnitude adds no information.

B. The larger ST-segment depression indicates more severe myocardial ischemia.

C. The magnitude cannot be interpreted unless lead II is monitored simultaneously.

D. The change identifies the coronary territory but does not indicate severity.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Both tracings only cross a binary ischemia threshold, so the magnitude adds no information.

Myocardial ischemia is characterized by ST-segment elevation or depression of at least 1 mm and T-wave inversions.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Both tracings only cross a binary ischemia threshold, so the magnitude adds no information.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate ST-depression magnitude to myocardial ischemia severity.

**B — correct:** The larger ST-segment depression indicates more severe myocardial ischemia.

The degree of ST-segment depression parallels the severity of myocardial ischemia.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Relate ST-depression magnitude to myocardial ischemia severity.

**C:** The magnitude cannot be interpreted unless lead II is monitored simultaneously.

Traditionally, two-lead monitoring with leads II and V5 has been standard.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The magnitude cannot be interpreted unless lead II is monitored simultaneously.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate ST-depression magnitude to myocardial ischemia severity.

**D:** The change identifies the coronary territory but does not indicate severity.

The V5 lead reflects ischemia in left-ventricular myocardium supplied by the left anterior descending artery.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The change identifies the coronary territory but does not indicate severity.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Relate ST-depression magnitude to myocardial ischemia severity.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 117, passage 232760

## S02-Q016

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

A patient had PCI 3 years ago and now requires noncardiac surgery. Surgical bleeding risk permits aspirin. What is the preferred perioperative antiplatelet plan?

A. Continue dual antiplatelet therapy because surgery is within 30 days of stent placement.

B. Treat the patient as being in the highest-risk first 6 weeks after stenting.

C. Continue aspirin perioperatively.

D. Delay surgery for 1 month while dual antiplatelet therapy is completed.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Continue dual antiplatelet therapy because surgery is within 30 days of stent placement.

If time-sensitive surgery is required within 30 days of bare-metal stent placement, DAPT generally continues unless bleeding risk outweighs stent-thrombosis prevention.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue dual antiplatelet therapy because surgery is within 30 days of stent placement.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply perioperative aspirin continuation principles after remote coronary intervention.

**B:** Treat the patient as being in the highest-risk first 6 weeks after stenting.

The risk of adverse cardiovascular outcomes after coronary stenting appears highest during the first 6 weeks.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Treat the patient as being in the highest-risk first 6 weeks after stenting.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply perioperative aspirin continuation principles after remote coronary intervention.

**C — correct:** Continue aspirin perioperatively.

When PCI occurred at least 12 months earlier, aspirin should be continued perioperatively if bleeding risk allows.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply perioperative aspirin continuation principles after remote coronary intervention.

**D:** Delay surgery for 1 month while dual antiplatelet therapy is completed.

After elective PCI with a bare-metal stent for chronic coronary disease, noncardiac surgery should generally be delayed at least 1 month while DAPT is continued.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Delay surgery for 1 month while dual antiplatelet therapy is completed.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply perioperative aspirin continuation principles after remote coronary intervention.

**Evidence sources:**

- 95__Perioperative_Management_of_Antiplatelet_Therapy.html — PDF p. 1, passage 316759

## S02-Q017

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

Which perioperative scenario most strongly supports continuing a patient's established aspirin therapy?

A. Continuing aspirin through a high-bleeding-risk operation despite a decision to interrupt it.

B. Stopping aspirin for a minimal-bleeding-risk procedure.

C. Starting aspirin in a previous non-user solely to reduce perioperative cardiovascular events.

D. Carotid endarterectomy after a recent ischemic stroke.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Continuing aspirin through a high-bleeding-risk operation despite a decision to interrupt it.

If aspirin interruption is chosen for a high-bleeding-risk noncardiac operation, interruption for 7 days is suggested and resumption is typically within 24 hours after bleeding risk passes.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continuing aspirin through a high-bleeding-risk operation despite a decision to interrupt it.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify clinical situations in which perioperative aspirin continuation may be appropriate.

**B:** Stopping aspirin for a minimal-bleeding-risk procedure.

For minimal or low/moderate bleeding-risk surgery, perioperative aspirin continuation is listed as a clinical situation for continuation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Stopping aspirin for a minimal-bleeding-risk procedure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify clinical situations in which perioperative aspirin continuation may be appropriate.

**C:** Starting aspirin in a previous non-user solely to reduce perioperative cardiovascular events.

Starting aspirin before surgery in previous non-users to reduce perioperative cardiovascular events is not recommended.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Starting aspirin in a previous non-user solely to reduce perioperative cardiovascular events.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify clinical situations in which perioperative aspirin continuation may be appropriate.

**D — correct:** Carotid endarterectomy after a recent ischemic stroke.

Aspirin continuation may be appropriate for carotid endarterectomy or recent acute coronary syndrome or stroke.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Identify clinical situations in which perioperative aspirin continuation may be appropriate.

**Evidence sources:**

- 95__Perioperative_Management_of_Antiplatelet_Therapy.html — PDF p. 1, passage 316757

## S02-Q018

Domain 2 · Heart failure and cardiomyopathy

Which definition encompasses both the systolic and diastolic pathways by which left-ventricular dysfunction produces heart failure?

A. Failure to eject enough blood for metabolic demand or failure to fill without elevated pressure.

B. Failure of a chamber to collect blood effectively at a normal filling pressure.

C. Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction with ventricular and arterial stiffening.

D. Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction with a reduced ejection fraction.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Failure to eject enough blood for metabolic demand or failure to fill without elevated pressure.

Heart failure occurs when the LV cannot eject sufficiently to meet metabolic demands or cannot fill without elevated pressure.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Define the functional distinction between heart-failure systolic ejection and diastolic filling.

**B:** Failure of a chamber to collect blood effectively at a normal filling pressure.

Diastolic function is the ability of a cardiac chamber to collect blood effectively at normal filling pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Failure of a chamber to collect blood effectively at a normal filling pressure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Define the functional distinction between heart-failure systolic ejection and diastolic filling.

**C:** Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction with ventricular and arterial stiffening.

Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction and ventricular and arterial stiffening are common in HFpEF.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction with ventricular and arterial stiffening.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Define the functional distinction between heart-failure systolic ejection and diastolic filling.

**D:** Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction with a reduced ejection fraction.

Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is characterized by left-ventricular systolic dysfunction.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction with a reduced ejection fraction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Define the functional distinction between heart-failure systolic ejection and diastolic filling.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 841, passage 244672

## S02-Q019

Domain 2 · Heart failure and cardiomyopathy

A patient has clinical heart failure and a left-ventricular ejection fraction of 52%. Using the passage-defined threshold, which phenotype applies?

A. Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.

B. Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction.

C. The phenotype cannot be assigned because diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

D. Heart failure without an ejection-fraction phenotype because filling and ejection can both be impaired.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.

Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is defined in this passage as an ejection fraction of 40% or less.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the passage-defined ejection-fraction threshold for HFpEF.

**B — correct:** Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction.

HFpEF is diagnosed in patients with an ejection fraction of 50% or more.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the passage-defined ejection-fraction threshold for HFpEF.

**C:** The phenotype cannot be assigned because diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

Diastolic dysfunction is present in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The phenotype cannot be assigned because diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the passage-defined ejection-fraction threshold for HFpEF.

**D:** Heart failure without an ejection-fraction phenotype because filling and ejection can both be impaired.

Heart failure can result from structural or functional impairment of ventricular filling or ejection.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Heart failure without an ejection-fraction phenotype because filling and ejection can both be impaired.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the passage-defined ejection-fraction threshold for HFpEF.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 214, passage 233176

## S02-Q020

Domain 2 · Heart failure and cardiomyopathy

In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, left-ventricular systolic pressure rises after a premature ventricular contraction while arterial pulse pressure falls. Which change best explains this response?

A. An outflow obstruction gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.

B. Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

C. The left-ventricular outflow obstruction gradient increases after the premature beat.

D. The hypertrophic-cardiomyopathy murmur becomes louder with Valsalva or standing.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: C**

**A:** An outflow obstruction gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.

A left-ventricular outflow obstruction gradient of 30 mm Hg or more is clinically significant.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An outflow obstruction gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret the increase in outflow gradient after a premature ventricular contraction in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

**B:** Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret the increase in outflow gradient after a premature ventricular contraction in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

**C — correct:** The left-ventricular outflow obstruction gradient increases after the premature beat.

## S02-Q050

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Obstructive shock.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Classify cardiogenic shock by low cardiac output and increased systemic vascular resistance.

**D:** Hypovolemic shock.

Hypovolemic shock is associated with low cardiac output and increased peripheral resistance.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Hypovolemic shock.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Classify cardiogenic shock by low cardiac output and increased systemic vascular resistance.

## S02-Q055

Domain 4 · Respiratory failure and ventilation

Which statement defines the effect of auto-PEEP specifically in terms of end-expiratory lung volume?

A. Management may include more expiratory time and a lower tidal volume or respiratory rate.

B. End-expiratory lung volume remains larger than it would be without auto-PEEP.

C. Dynamic hyperinflation may reduce venous return.

D. Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete emptying.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Management may include more expiratory time and a lower tidal volume or respiratory rate.

Increasing expiratory time and reducing tidal volume or respiratory rate should be considered to address auto-PEEP.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Management may include more expiratory time and a lower tidal volume or respiratory rate.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret auto-PEEP as increased end-expiratory lung volume after incomplete emptying.

**B — correct:** End-expiratory lung volume remains larger than it would be without auto-PEEP.

Auto-PEEP leaves end-expiratory lung volumes larger than without auto-PEEP.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Interpret auto-PEEP as increased end-expiratory lung volume after incomplete emptying.

**C:** Dynamic hyperinflation may reduce venous return.

Auto-PEEP carries risk of dynamic lung hyperinflation and reduced venous return.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Dynamic hyperinflation may reduce venous return.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret auto-PEEP as increased end-expiratory lung volume after incomplete emptying.

**D:** Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete emptying.

Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete lung emptying characteristic of auto-PEEP.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete emptying.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret auto-PEEP as increased end-expiratory lung volume after incomplete emptying.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1409, passage 287087

## S02-Q056

Domain 4 · Respiratory failure and ventilation

What does the ratio VD/VT represent?

A. Respiratory rate divided by tidal volume.

B. Tidal volume divided by physiologic dead-space volume.

C. Physiologic dead-space volume divided by tidal volume.

D. Minute ventilation divided by tidal volume.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Respiratory rate divided by tidal volume.

The derived dead-space relationship is VD/VT, not respiratory rate divided by tidal volume.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Respiratory rate divided by tidal volume.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Use dead-space fraction to distinguish alveolar ventilation from minute ventilation.

**B:** Tidal volume divided by physiologic dead-space volume.

The derived dead-space relationship is VD/VT, not its inverse VT/VD.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Tidal volume divided by physiologic dead-space volume.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Use dead-space fraction to distinguish alveolar ventilation from minute ventilation.

**C — correct:** Physiologic dead-space volume divided by tidal volume.

VD/VT is the ratio of physiologic dead space to tidal volume.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Use dead-space fraction to distinguish alveolar ventilation from minute ventilation.

**D:** Minute ventilation divided by tidal volume.

The derived dead-space relationship is VD/VT, not minute ventilation divided by tidal volume.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Minute ventilation divided by tidal volume.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Use dead-space fraction to distinguish alveolar ventilation from minute ventilation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 1766, passage 314687
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 326, passage 282017
- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 788, passage 186129

## S02-Q057

Domain 4 · Multiorgan failure and ICU care

A septic patient develops dysfunction in neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic systems. Which score summarizes these domains?

A. SIRS features can occur in trauma, surgery, burns, and pancreatitis.

B. Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction from dysregulated host response, often involving MODS.

C. Sepsis organ dysfunction includes hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, or oliguria.

D. The SOFA score assesses neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: D**

**A:** SIRS features can occur in trauma, surgery, burns, and pancreatitis.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “SIRS features can occur in trauma, surgery, burns, and pancreatitis.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize SOFA organ-system domains used to quantify physiologic deterioration.

**B:** Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction from dysregulated host response, often involving MODS.

Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction resulting from a dysregulated host response to infection, and mortality is typically a result of multiple-organ dysfunction syndrome.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction from dysregulated host response, often involving MODS.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize SOFA organ-system domains used to quantify physiologic deterioration.

**C:** Sepsis organ dysfunction includes hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, or oliguria.

Sepsis organ dysfunction includes clinical or laboratory abnormalities such as hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, or oliguria.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Sepsis organ dysfunction includes hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, or oliguria.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize SOFA organ-system domains used to quantify physiologic deterioration.

**D — correct:** The SOFA score assesses neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function.

The SOFA score assesses neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize SOFA organ-system domains used to quantify physiologic deterioration.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 3052, passage 295141

## S02-Q058

Domain 4 · Multiorgan failure and ICU care

B. Light sedation and daily awakening trials can shorten ventilation and ICU stay.

D. ICU delirium is distinguished by acute onset, fluctuation, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness.

**Answer: A**

Reorientation, day-night-cycle maintenance, and noise reduction can reduce delirium and confusion.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Reorientation, day-night maintenance, and noise reduction can reduce delirium and confusion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize CAM-ICU as a structured screening instrument for ICU delirium.

**D:** ICU delirium is distinguished by acute onset, fluctuation, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness.

ICU delirium is distinguished by acute onset, a fluctuating course, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “ICU delirium is distinguished by acute onset, fluctuation, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize CAM-ICU as a structured screening instrument for ICU delirium.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 4859, passage 251801

## S02-Q069

Domain 5 · Standards and basic monitors

A pulse-oximeter site loses a reliable pulsatile signal during peripheral vasoconstriction. Which response directly addresses that problem?

A. Evaluate for pediatric endobronchial intubation despite no tube-position concern.

B. Rely on supplemental oxygen to make desaturation appear sooner during apnea.

C. Assess another vascular bed for satisfactory pulsatile perfusion.

D. Use pulse oximetry to confirm that ventilation is adequate.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Evaluate for pediatric endobronchial intubation despite no tube-position concern.

Pulse oximetry may be more sensitive than capnography for unrecognized endobronchial intubation in children.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Evaluate for pediatric endobronchial intubation despite no tube-position concern.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret pulse-oximeter limitations in dyshemoglobinemia and poor perfusion.

**B:** Rely on supplemental oxygen to make desaturation appear sooner during apnea.

Supplemental oxygen can permit apnea for minutes before pulse-oximeter desaturation appears.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Rely on supplemental oxygen to make desaturation appear sooner during apnea.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret pulse-oximeter limitations in dyshemoglobinemia and poor perfusion.

**C — correct:** Assess another vascular bed for satisfactory pulsatile perfusion.

Pulse-oximeter reliability depends on residual cardiac pulsatility, and alternative vascular beds may need assessment when perfusion is poor.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Interpret pulse-oximeter limitations in dyshemoglobinemia and poor perfusion.

**D:** Use pulse oximetry to confirm that ventilation is adequate.

Pulse oximetry is a poor indicator of adequate ventilation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use pulse oximetry to confirm that ventilation is adequate.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret pulse-oximeter limitations in dyshemoglobinemia and poor perfusion.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 2103, passage 246851

Cardiac output can also be estimated using impedance cardiography and pulse-contour analysis.

Oxygen consumption is measured by collecting exhaled gases and measuring their volume and oxygen concentration.

## S02-Q075

C. Circuit disconnection alone.

D. Cardiac arrest alone.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Tube malposition alone.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Differentiate abrupt capnogram loss from disconnection versus pulmonary blood-flow loss.

**C:** Circuit disconnection alone.

The differential includes airway, circulatory, thromboembolic, arrest, and circuit causes rather than circuit disconnection alone.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Circuit disconnection alone.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Differentiate abrupt capnogram loss from disconnection versus pulmonary blood-flow loss.

**D:** Cardiac arrest alone.

The differential includes tube malposition, severe hypotension, pulmonary embolism, and circuit disconnection in addition to cardiac arrest.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Cardiac arrest alone.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Differentiate abrupt capnogram loss from disconnection versus pulmonary blood-flow loss.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 2109, passage 246860

## S02-Q088

An intracranial mass expands after cerebrospinal-fluid compensation is exhausted. Which volume-conservation principle explains why ICP now rises?

B. The relevant principle is that normal ICP is 5 to 15 mm Hg.

C. Normal compensation remains unlimited despite continued mass expansion.

**Answer: A**

An increase in one intracranial-volume component must be offset by a decrease in another to prevent ICP rise.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply the Monro-Kellie doctrine to predict increased ICP when CSF compensation is exhausted.

**B:** The relevant principle is that normal ICP is 5 to 15 mm Hg.

Normal ICP is usually 5 to 15 mm Hg.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The relevant principle is that normal ICP is 5 to 15 mm Hg.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the Monro-Kellie doctrine to predict increased ICP when CSF compensation is exhausted.

**C:** Normal compensation remains unlimited despite continued mass expansion.

Changes in intracranial components are well compensated during normal physiology.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Normal compensation remains unlimited despite continued mass expansion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the Monro-Kellie doctrine to predict increased ICP when CSF compensation is exhausted.

**D:** Every small intracranial-volume increase causes the same large ICP rise even before compensation is exhausted.

Normal compensation can reach a point where a small intracranial-volume change causes a large ICP increase.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Every small intracranial-volume increase causes the same large ICP rise even before compensation is exhausted.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the Monro-Kellie doctrine to predict increased ICP when CSF compensation is exhausted.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 286, passage 233461

## S02-Q089

## S02-Q124

Domain 9 · Pediatric physiology and pharmacology

Which four-factor infant respiratory profile best explains rapid desaturation during anesthetic apnea?

A. Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

B. Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, an immature biphasic hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

C. Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

D. Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, an immature hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

The evidence supports reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, an immature biphasic hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption—not the opposite profile.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate pediatric oxygen consumption, functional residual capacity, and airway anatomy to predict rapid desaturation.

**B — correct:** Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, an immature biphasic hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate pediatric oxygen consumption, functional residual capacity, and airway anatomy to predict rapid desaturation.

**C:** Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

The evidence supports an immature hypoxic response and high oxygen consumption—not a mature sustained response with low oxygen consumption.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate pediatric oxygen consumption, functional residual capacity, and airway anatomy to predict rapid desaturation.

**D:** Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, an immature hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

The evidence supports reduced FRC and smaller high-resistance airways with high oxygen consumption—not increased FRC and larger low-resistance airways.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, an immature hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate pediatric oxygen consumption, functional residual capacity, and airway anatomy to predict rapid desaturation.

Which direct effects of general anesthesia create a physiologic predisposition to rapid desaturation during pediatric emergence?

B. Active upper-respiratory infection increases airway reactivity, atelectasis, and secretions, worsening desaturation.

**B:** Active upper-respiratory infection increases airway reactivity, atelectasis, and secretions, worsening desaturation.

An active upper-respiratory infection increases airway reactivity, atelectasis, and secretions, but this is a patient-specific respiratory condition rather than a direct effect of general anesthesia.

Reduction of functional residual capacity and hypoxic and hypercapnic ventilatory drive, together with altered hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, is the best answer because these are direct anesthetic effects that predispose a child to rapid emergence desaturation.

## S02-Q131

Domain 9 · Neonatal anesthesia

A severely hypoxemic neonate has pulmonary hypertension. During immediate ventilatory stabilization, the goal is to reduce pulmonary vascular resistance by correcting hypoxemia and inducing respiratory alkalosis. Which combined intervention accomplishes this?

A. Use hyperventilation to induce respiratory alkalosis together with 100% oxygen.

B. Ventilate with small tidal volumes and low inflating pressures to avoid a contralateral pneumothorax.

C. Design the anesthetic to minimize sympathetic discharge, which may exacerbate pulmonary hypertension.

D. Use nitric oxide or high-frequency oscillatory ventilation during surgical repair.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Use hyperventilation to induce respiratory alkalosis together with 100% oxygen.

Hyperventilation to induce respiratory alkalosis together with 100% oxygen is the best answer because it directly accomplishes both mechanisms specified in the stem and may decrease pulmonary vascular resistance.

**B:** Ventilate with small tidal volumes and low inflating pressures to avoid a contralateral pneumothorax.

Small tidal volumes and low inflating pressures help avoid contralateral pneumothorax, but they do not deliberately induce respiratory alkalosis while maximizing inspired oxygen.

**C:** Design the anesthetic to minimize sympathetic discharge, which may exacerbate pulmonary hypertension.

Minimizing sympathetic discharge may avoid exacerbating pulmonary hypertension, but it does not itself accomplish the paired oxygenation and alkalosis goals specified in the stem.

**D:** Use nitric oxide or high-frequency oscillatory ventilation during surgical repair.

Inhaled nitric oxide and high-frequency oscillatory ventilation may be used during surgical repair, but this option does not describe the immediate combined intervention that corrects hypoxemia and induces respiratory alkalosis.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 658, passage 185551

## S02-Q150

Domain 10 · Renal disease

A dialysis-dependent patient is scheduled for elective surgery. Which complete perioperative plan is best?

A. Dialyze on the operative morning, sample potassium immediately, and use nondominant upper-extremity veins for access.

B. Dialyze immediately before surgery, supplement potassium from the first postdialysis sample, and preserve access veins.

C. Dialyze the day before, check morning electrolytes after equilibration, optimize volume, and preserve potential fistula veins.

D. Dialyze the day before, omit morning electrolytes, and use potential fistula veins for routine blood draws.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Dialyze on the operative morning, sample potassium immediately, and use nondominant upper-extremity veins for access.

Same-day dialysis with immediate postdialysis potassium sampling and venipuncture in potential fistula veins conflicts with day-before equilibration, morning electrolyte assessment, and access preservation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Dialyze on the operative morning, sample potassium immediately, and use nondominant upper-extremity veins for access.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate dialysis timing, potassium concentration, volume status, and access preservation when preparing a dialysis-dependent patient for surgery.

**B:** Dialyze immediately before surgery, supplement potassium from the first postdialysis sample, and preserve access veins.

Immediate postdialysis potassium sampling may be falsely low; the supported plan allows equilibration, checks morning electrolytes, and preserves potential fistula veins.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Dialyze immediately before surgery, supplement potassium from the first postdialysis sample, and preserve access veins.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate dialysis timing, potassium concentration, volume status, and access preservation when preparing a dialysis-dependent patient for surgery.

**C — correct:** Dialyze the day before, check morning electrolytes after equilibration, optimize volume, and preserve potential fistula veins.

For elective surgery, perform dialysis the day before to allow fluid and electrolyte equilibration, check electrolytes—including potassium—on the morning of surgery, and preserve potential dialysis-fistula sites by avoiding venipuncture or blood draws from the nondominant upper-extremity access veins.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate dialysis timing, potassium concentration, volume status, and access preservation when preparing a dialysis-dependent patient for surgery.

**D:** Dialyze the day before, omit morning electrolytes, and use potential fistula veins for routine blood draws.

Day-before dialysis without morning electrolyte assessment or dialysis-access preservation omits required elements of the supported elective plan.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Dialyze the day before, omit morning electrolytes, and use potential fistula veins for routine blood draws.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate dialysis timing, potassium concentration, volume status, and access preservation when preparing a dialysis-dependent patient for surgery.

## S02-Q197

Domain 12 · Neuraxial anesthesia

Immediately after an epidural injection, a patient reports a metallic taste and tinnitus and then develops neurologic or cardiovascular deterioration. Which complication best explains this presentation?

A. Expected prevention of local-anesthetic toxicity through incremental injection and a marker-containing test dose.

B. Postdural-puncture headache after lumbar puncture, which is less frequent with a smaller-gauge needle.

C. High spinal block after an epidural dose enters the intrathecal space, causing respiratory compromise and unconsciousness.

D. Neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity from epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: D**

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Postdural-puncture headache after lumbar puncture, which is less frequent with a smaller-gauge needle.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity and distinguish it from postdural-puncture headache or high spinal block.

**C:** High spinal block after an epidural dose enters the intrathecal space, causing respiratory compromise and unconsciousness.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “High spinal block after an epidural dose enters the intrathecal space, causing respiratory compromise and unconsciousness.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity and distinguish it from postdural-puncture headache or high spinal block.

**D — correct:** Neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity from epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection.

Neuraxial LAST can follow epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection and may present with tinnitus or metallic taste before seizures or cardiac arrest.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity and distinguish it from postdural-puncture headache or high spinal block.

## S02-Q198

Domain 12 · Neuraxial anesthesia

Three days after prolonged epidural catheterization, a patient develops severe localized backache, fever, and new leg weakness. Which evaluation and treatment pathway is most appropriate?

A. Suspect epidural abscess, obtain prompt MRI, and arrange urgent decompression/laminectomy when neurologic signs are present, with directed antibiotics.

B. Start directed antibiotics for a routine 4–6 weeks without tailoring therapy or considering neurologic decompression.

C. Treat the presentation as a post-neuraxial hematoma without prioritizing infectious evaluation or MRI.

D. Identify Staphylococcus aureus as the likely organism and defer imaging or decompression until cultures are finalized.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Suspect epidural abscess, obtain prompt MRI, and arrange urgent decompression/laminectomy when neurologic signs are present, with directed antibiotics.

An epidural abscess after neuraxial placement typically follows prolonged epidural catheterization over 1–4 days; severe backache and fever should prompt MRI, because untreated infection can progress to neurologic deficits and requires early diagnosis with prompt laminectomy when neurologic signs appear.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate fever, neurologic deficit, back pain, and timing after neuraxial placement to recognize spinal epidural abscess and prioritize imaging and treatment.

**B:** Start directed antibiotics for a routine 4–6 weeks without tailoring therapy or considering neurologic decompression.

Directed antibiotic therapy is mandatory, and treatment usually lasts four to six weeks without complications.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Start directed antibiotics for a routine 4–6 weeks without tailoring therapy or considering neurologic decompression.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate fever, neurologic deficit, back pain, and timing after neuraxial placement to recognize spinal epidural abscess and prioritize imaging and treatment.

**C:** Treat the presentation as a post-neuraxial hematoma without prioritizing infectious evaluation or MRI.

Epidural abscess after neuraxial blockade is rare and usually follows epidural catheterization rather than spinal anesthesia alone.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Treat the presentation as a post-neuraxial hematoma without prioritizing infectious evaluation or MRI.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate fever, neurologic deficit, back pain, and timing after neuraxial placement to recognize spinal epidural abscess and prioritize imaging and treatment.

**D:** Identify Staphylococcus aureus as the likely organism and defer imaging or decompression until cultures are finalized.

Staphylococcus aureus is the most common causative organism in epidural abscess.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Identify Staphylococcus aureus as the likely organism and defer imaging or decompression until cultures are finalized.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate fever, neurologic deficit, back pain, and timing after neuraxial placement to recognize spinal epidural abscess and prioritize imaging and treatment.

## S02-Q200

A patient with limited respiratory reserve needs analgesia for shoulder surgery, and preservation of postoperative hemidiaphragmatic function is important. Which regional strategy is best supported?

A. Choose supraclavicular block as fully phrenic-sparing because its phrenic palsy incidence is lower than interscalene block.

B. Use posterior suprascapular plus axillary nerve block for major shoulder surgery because it provides superior analgesia to interscalene block.

C. Use a low-volume superior trunk block for effective analgesia with preserved hemidiaphragmatic function; a selective anterior suprascapular block is a noninferior alternative to interscalene block for ambulatory arthroscopy.

**Answer: C**

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select a phrenic-sparing brachial-plexus analgesic strategy when postoperative diaphragmatic function is important.

**D:** Choose traditional interscalene block because its historical ipsilateral diaphragmatic paresis rate was 100%.

Traditional interscalene block historically caused ipsilateral diaphragmatic paresis in 100% of patients.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Choose traditional interscalene block because its historical ipsilateral diaphragmatic paresis rate was 100%.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select a phrenic-sparing brachial-plexus analgesic strategy when postoperative diaphragmatic function is important.

## S02-Q203

After an epidural test dose containing approximately 15 mcg epinephrine, which hemodynamic response is expected if the catheter is intravascular?

## S02-Q213

Domain 13 · Respiratory physiology and gas exchange

During controlled ventilation, the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient widens while airway dead space is otherwise stable. Which interpretation is supported?

A. Use the inverse PaCO2–alveolar-ventilation relationship to interpret the widening gradient.

B. Substitute PETCO2 for PaCO2 in the Bohr equation because alveolar dead space is very small.

C. The PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient is a useful trend for detecting increased alveolar dead space.

D. Apply the derived VD/VT to minute ventilation or a single breath.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Use the inverse PaCO2–alveolar-ventilation relationship to interpret the widening gradient.

PaCO2 is inversely related to alveolar ventilation: PaCO2 = (VCO2/VA) × K; with constant metabolism, increasing alveolar ventilation lowers PaCO2.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use the inverse PaCO2–alveolar-ventilation relationship to interpret the widening gradient.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient as a trend for alveolar dead space during controlled ventilation.

**B:** Substitute PETCO2 for PaCO2 in the Bohr equation because alveolar dead space is very small.

In a healthy spontaneously breathing person, PETCO2 can substitute for PaCO2 in the Bohr equation because alveolar dead space is very small.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Substitute PETCO2 for PaCO2 in the Bohr equation because alveolar dead space is very small.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient as a trend for alveolar dead space during controlled ventilation.

**C — correct:** The PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient is a useful trend for detecting increased alveolar dead space.

The PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient is a clinically useful trend for detecting changes in alveolar dead space.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Interpret the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient as a trend for alveolar dead space during controlled ventilation.

**D:** Apply the derived VD/VT to minute ventilation or a single breath.

The derived VD/VT can be applied to minute ventilation or a single breath.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Apply the derived VD/VT to minute ventilation or a single breath.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient as a trend for alveolar dead space during controlled ventilation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 1766, passage 314687

## S02-Q214

During anesthesia, which integrated profile correctly relates ventilation, wasted ventilation, hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, and arterial oxygenation?

B. Chronic hypoxemia increases red-cell mass to improve oxygen-carrying capacity.

C. VD/VT identifies adequately ventilated areas with inadequate or absent pulmonary blood flow.

**C:** VD/VT identifies adequately ventilated areas with inadequate or absent pulmonary blood flow.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “VD/VT identifies adequately ventilated areas with inadequate or absent pulmonary blood flow.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify the physiologic effects of oxygen, carbon dioxide, and ventilation-perfusion matching on arterial oxygenation.

**D — correct:** PaCO2 reflects alveolar ventilation; VD/VT reflects wasted ventilation; and hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction diverts blood from hypoxic alveoli to help preserve V/Q matching and oxygenation.

PaCO2 reflects alveolar ventilation, the dead-space-to-tidal-volume ratio reflects wasted ventilation, and hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction diverts blood away from hypoxic alveoli to help preserve V/Q matching and arterial oxygenation.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Identify the physiologic effects of oxygen, carbon dioxide, and ventilation-perfusion matching on arterial oxygenation.

## S02-Q215

Domain 13 · Respiratory physiology and gas exchange

A patient becomes hypoxemic during anesthesia after a reduction in inspired oxygen. Which framework best integrates the major transport mechanisms that should be considered?

A. Consider low inspired PO2 or hypoventilation lowering PAO2, V/Q mismatch, right-to-left shunt, and diffusion limitation; hypoventilation, V/Q mismatch, and shunt account for most perioperative cases.

B. Classify ventilation reaching adequately ventilated but poorly perfused regions as wasted dead-space ventilation.

C. Attribute hypoxemia from a thickened alveolar-capillary barrier, such as interstitial lung disease, to diffusion limitation alone.

D. Use VD/VT as the measure of carbon-dioxide transfer efficiency.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Consider low inspired PO2 or hypoventilation lowering PAO2, V/Q mismatch, right-to-left shunt, and diffusion limitation; hypoventilation, V/Q mismatch, and shunt account for most perioperative cases.

Hypoxemia can arise from low inspired PO2 or hypoventilation lowering PAO2, V/Q mismatch, right-to-left shunt, or diffusion limitation, with the first three causes accounting for most perioperative hypoxemia.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate shunt, dead space, diffusion, and low inspired oxygen to interpret hypoxemia during anesthesia.

**B:** Classify ventilation reaching adequately ventilated but poorly perfused regions as wasted dead-space ventilation.

Ventilation to areas with adequate ventilation but inadequate blood flow is wasted or dead-space ventilation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Classify ventilation reaching adequately ventilated but poorly perfused regions as wasted dead-space ventilation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate shunt, dead space, diffusion, and low inspired oxygen to interpret hypoxemia during anesthesia.

**C:** Attribute hypoxemia from a thickened alveolar-capillary barrier, such as interstitial lung disease, to diffusion limitation alone.

Diffusion limitation plays a role when the alveolar-capillary barrier is thickened, such as in interstitial lung disease.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Attribute hypoxemia from a thickened alveolar-capillary barrier, such as interstitial lung disease, to diffusion limitation alone.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate shunt, dead space, diffusion, and low inspired oxygen to interpret hypoxemia during anesthesia.

**D:** Use VD/VT as the measure of carbon-dioxide transfer efficiency.

The dead-space-to-tidal-volume ratio reflects carbon-dioxide transfer efficiency.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use VD/VT as the measure of carbon-dioxide transfer efficiency.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate shunt, dead space, diffusion, and low inspired oxygen to interpret hypoxemia during anesthesia.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1398, passage 287038

## S02-Q216

Domain 13 · Respiratory physiology and gas exchange

A patient with low hemoglobin and reduced cardiac output has impaired systemic oxygen delivery. Which Fick-principle relationships should guide interpretation of delivery and extraction?

A. Recognize that hemorrhage lowers delivery through both reduced cardiac output and reduced arterial oxygen content.

B. DO2 = CO × CaO2; VO2 = CO × (CaO2 − CvO2); and oxygen extraction is (CaO2 − CvO2)/CaO2.

C. Calculate CaO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SaO2 plus PaO2 × 0.003.

D. Calculate CvO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SvO2 plus PvO2 × 0.003.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Recognize that hemorrhage lowers delivery through both reduced cardiac output and reduced arterial oxygen content.

Oxygen delivery can fall through reduced cardiac output or reduced arterial oxygen content during hemorrhagic shock.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Recognize that hemorrhage lowers delivery through both reduced cardiac output and reduced arterial oxygen content.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret oxygen-content and Fick-principle variables to quantify oxygen delivery and extraction.

**B — correct:** DO2 = CO × CaO2; VO2 = CO × (CaO2 − CvO2); and oxygen extraction is (CaO2 − CvO2)/CaO2.

Oxygen delivery equals cardiac output times arterial oxygen content (DO2 = CO × CaO2), while oxygen extraction is (CaO2−CvO2)/CaO2 and VO2 = CO × (CaO2−CvO2).

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Interpret oxygen-content and Fick-principle variables to quantify oxygen delivery and extraction.

**C:** Calculate CaO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SaO2 plus PaO2 × 0.003.

Arterial oxygen content is calculated as 1.36 × hemoglobin × arterial saturation plus dissolved oxygen (PaO2 × 0.003).

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Calculate CaO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SaO2 plus PaO2 × 0.003.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret oxygen-content and Fick-principle variables to quantify oxygen delivery and extraction.

**D:** Calculate CvO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SvO2 plus PvO2 × 0.003.

Mixed venous oxygen content is calculated as 1.36 × hemoglobin × mixed venous saturation plus dissolved oxygen (PvO2 × 0.003).

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Calculate CvO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SvO2 plus PvO2 × 0.003.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret oxygen-content and Fick-principle variables to quantify oxygen delivery and extraction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 1297, passage 245451
- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 2778, passage 316232

## S02-Q217

Domain 13 · Respiratory mechanics and ventilation

A patient with restrictive lung disease has low compliance and persistent hypoxemia during mechanical ventilation. Which ventilator strategy is best supported?

A. Delay extubation for tachypnea alone even when gas exchange and other assessments are satisfactory.

B. Expect rapid, shallow breathing during weaning as the primary ventilator adjustment.

C. Use low tidal volumes, limit plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O, and carefully titrate PEEP with an inspiratory pause to optimize compliance.

**Answer: C**

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Expect rapid, shallow breathing during weaning as the primary ventilator adjustment.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply pressure-volume relationships and compliance changes to select ventilator adjustments in restrictive disease.

**C — correct:** Use low tidal volumes, limit plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O, and carefully titrate PEEP with an inspiratory pause to optimize compliance.

In restrictive lung disease, low compliance may require low tidal volumes, PEEP, and plateau-pressure limitation; PEEP should be titrated with inspiratory pause to optimize compliance.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply pressure-volume relationships and compliance changes to select ventilator adjustments in restrictive disease.

**D:** Use mechanical ventilation and PEEP whenever persistent hypoxemia occurs, without further compliance or pressure assessment.

Persistent hypoxemia in restrictive disease may require mechanical ventilation and PEEP.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use mechanical ventilation and PEEP whenever persistent hypoxemia occurs, without further compliance or pressure assessment.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply pressure-volume relationships and compliance changes to select ventilator adjustments in restrictive disease.
