## S02-Q219

Domain 13 · Respiratory mechanics and ventilation

A mechanically ventilated patient’s expiratory flow remains above zero when the next breath begins. Which adjustment best permits complete lung emptying?

A. Recognize auto-PEEP and increase expiratory time while reducing tidal volume or respiratory rate.

B. Measure auto-PEEP with end-expiratory flow interruption on an intensive-care ventilator.

C. Use the proportionality of auto-PEEP to tidal volume and its inverse relation to expiratory time as the adjustment itself.

D. Recognize that auto-PEEP increases end-expiratory volume and risks hyperinflation and reduced venous return.

**Answer: A**

**C:** Use the proportionality of auto-PEEP to tidal volume and its inverse relation to expiratory time as the adjustment itself.

Auto-PEEP is directly proportional to tidal volume and inversely proportional to expiratory time.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use the proportionality of auto-PEEP to tidal volume and its inverse relation to expiratory time as the adjustment itself.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select ventilator adjustments after recognizing auto-PEEP from persistent end-expiratory flow to permit complete lung emptying.

**D:** Recognize that auto-PEEP increases end-expiratory volume and risks hyperinflation and reduced venous return.

Auto-PEEP increases end-expiratory lung volume and risks dynamic hyperinflation and reduced venous return.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Recognize that auto-PEEP increases end-expiratory volume and risks hyperinflation and reduced venous return.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select ventilator adjustments after recognizing auto-PEEP from persistent end-expiratory flow to permit complete lung emptying.

## S02-Q236

A patient has severe laryngeal trauma with disrupted airway anatomy. Which approach may be the most prudent way to secure the airway?

Video laryngoscopy can improve Cormack and Lehane grade 1–2 views.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize that videolaryngoscopy can improve Cormack-Lehane grade 1–2 views.

**D:** Video laryngoscopy can serve as a rescue technique in a difficult airway.

Video laryngoscopy can serve as a rescue technique in a patient with a difficult airway.

## S02-Q008

Domain 1 · Difficult-airway rescue

Under the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines, a second-generation supraglottic airway provides satisfactory ventilation after failed intubation. The operation is essential, but tracheal intubation itself is not. What may be considered?

A. Continue the operation using the supraglottic airway device.

B. Perform blind tracheal intubation through the supraglottic airway.

C. Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient because proceeding is not essential.

D. Make one flexible-bronchoscope intubation attempt through the supraglottic airway.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Continue the operation using the supraglottic airway device.

If proceeding is essential and ventilation through the supraglottic airway device is satisfactory, continuing with the supraglottic airway device may be considered.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply the DAS 2025 conditional pathway for continuing an essential procedure with effective supraglottic ventilation.

**B:** Perform blind tracheal intubation through the supraglottic airway.

The guideline states plainly: 'Blind tracheal intubation through a supraglottic airway device is not recommended.' This option is explicitly excluded regardless of the surgical urgency described in the stem.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Perform blind tracheal intubation through the supraglottic airway.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the DAS 2025 conditional pathway for continuing an essential procedure with effective supraglottic ventilation.

**C:** Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient because proceeding is not essential.

If proceeding is not essential after effective supraglottic-airway oxygenation, the default is to discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient because proceeding is not essential.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the DAS 2025 conditional pathway for continuing an essential procedure with effective supraglottic ventilation.

**D:** Make one flexible-bronchoscope intubation attempt through the supraglottic airway.

If tracheal intubation is essential, one flexible-bronchoscope intubation attempt through the supraglottic airway device may be considered.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Make one flexible-bronchoscope intubation attempt through the supraglottic airway.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply the DAS 2025 conditional pathway for continuing an essential procedure with effective supraglottic ventilation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 399, passage 233940
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 10, passage 317285

## S02-Q009

Domain 1 · Extubation and complications

A patient with a previously difficult airway is fully responsive and maintaining spontaneous ventilation before planned tube removal. Which physiologic rationale supports an awake extubation approach?

A. Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks obstruction and hypoventilation.

B. An awake patient more easily maintains a patent airway because pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes recover.

C. An extubation plan should include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.

D. Extubation during a light stage-II plane increases laryngospasm and other airway complications.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks obstruction and hypoventilation.

Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks upper-airway obstruction and hypoventilation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks obstruction and hypoventilation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Explain why an awake patient more easily maintains airway patency after extubation.

**B — correct:** An awake patient more easily maintains a patent airway because pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes recover.

An awake patient more easily maintains a patent airway because pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes recover.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Explain why an awake patient more easily maintains airway patency after extubation.

**C:** An extubation plan should include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.

An extubation plan should be formulated in advance and include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An extubation plan should include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Explain why an awake patient more easily maintains airway patency after extubation.

**D:** Extubation during a light stage-II plane increases laryngospasm and other airway complications.

Extubation during a light stage-II plane of anesthesia increases laryngospasm and other airway complications and should be avoided.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Extubation during a light stage-II plane increases laryngospasm and other airway complications.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Explain why an awake patient more easily maintains airway patency after extubation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1512, passage 287594

## S02-Q010

Domain 1 · Extubation and complications

Immediately after extubation, jaw thrust with CPAP has been tried but the upper airway remains obstructed. What next noninvasive airway adjunct is supported before tracheal reintubation?

A. Delay airway opening until the cause of obstruction has been identified and treated.

B. Proceed immediately to tracheal reintubation without another noninvasive airway-opening measure.

C. Insert an oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway.

D. Continue jaw thrust with CPAP as the only possible noninvasive intervention.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Delay airway opening until the cause of obstruction has been identified and treated.

Miller places identification and treatment of the cause after the upper airway has been opened and adequate ventilation ensured; airway opening therefore remains the first priority.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Delay airway opening until the cause of obstruction has been identified and treated.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize oral-airway insertion as the next step when jaw thrust with CPAP is ineffective.

**B:** Proceed immediately to tracheal reintubation without another noninvasive airway-opening measure.

Miller directs clinicians to attempt noninvasive airway-opening measures before tracheal reintubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Proceed immediately to tracheal reintubation without another noninvasive airway-opening measure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize oral-airway insertion as the next step when jaw thrust with CPAP is ineffective.

**C — correct:** Insert an oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway.

Miller places oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway insertion after CPAP is found ineffective.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize oral-airway insertion as the next step when jaw thrust with CPAP is ineffective.

**D:** Continue jaw thrust with CPAP as the only possible noninvasive intervention.

Miller's Anesthesia, 10th edition states that an obstructed upper airway requires immediate attention, that noninvasive measures should precede reintubation, and that jaw thrust with CPAP is often sufficient to open an upper airway obstructed by decreased pharyngeal muscle tone.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue jaw thrust with CPAP as the only possible noninvasive intervention.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize oral-airway insertion as the next step when jaw thrust with CPAP is ineffective.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 27, passage 232366

## S02-Q021

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

A patient with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy develops hypotension after induction. Which hemodynamic strategy best addresses the obstructive physiology?

A. For mitral stenosis, maintain heart rate at the low end of normal and avoid and treat tachycardia.

B. For aortic stenosis, maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.

C. For coronary artery disease, control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.

D. Increase afterload and decrease contractility, treating hypotension aggressively with an alpha-adrenergic agonist.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: D**

**A:** For mitral stenosis, maintain heart rate at the low end of normal and avoid and treat tachycardia.

In mitral stenosis, heart rate should be maintained at the low end of normal, avoiding and treating tachycardia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For mitral stenosis, maintain heart rate at the low end of normal and avoid and treat tachycardia.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize an alternative supported valve-hemodynamic relationship.

**B:** For aortic stenosis, maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.

In patients with aortic stenosis, anesthetic management should include maintenance of sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and maintenance of adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For aortic stenosis, maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize an alternative supported valve-hemodynamic relationship.

**C:** For coronary artery disease, control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.

In coronary artery disease, the hemodynamic goals are to control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For coronary artery disease, control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize an alternative supported valve-hemodynamic relationship.

**D — correct:** Increase afterload and decrease contractility, treating hypotension aggressively with an alpha-adrenergic agonist.

In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, hemodynamic management should increase afterload and decrease contractility, treating hypotension aggressively with α-adrenergic agonists.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize an alternative supported valve-hemodynamic relationship.

## S02-Q022

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

A patient with high-risk aortic stenosis and LV dysfunction is being considered for neuraxial sympathectomy. Which management plan best limits hemodynamic deterioration?

A. Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators, meticulously maintain preload and afterload, and avoid an abrupt SVR decrease with sympathectomy.

B. For mitral stenosis, avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.

C. If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs before induction, postpone surgery if possible until rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved.

D. For severe pulmonary hypertension, maintain SVR and preload or venous return, treat hypoxemia and hypercarbia, and avoid myocardial depression.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators, meticulously maintain preload and afterload, and avoid an abrupt SVR decrease with sympathectomy.

In high-risk aortic stenosis with LV dysfunction, management should avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators, meticulously maintain preload and afterload, and avoid an abrupt decrease in SVR with sympathectomy.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply an alternative supported aortic-stenosis plan.

**B:** For mitral stenosis, avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.

In mitral stenosis, tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right ventricular afterload should all be avoided.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For mitral stenosis, avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply an alternative supported aortic-stenosis plan.

**C:** If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs before induction, postpone surgery if possible until rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved.

If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs prior to induction of anesthesia, surgery should be postponed, if possible, until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs before induction, postpone surgery if possible until rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply an alternative supported aortic-stenosis plan.

**D:** For severe pulmonary hypertension, maintain SVR and preload or venous return, treat hypoxemia and hypercarbia, and avoid myocardial depression.

In severe pulmonary hypertension, management should maintain SVR and preload/venous return, prevent or treat pain, hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis, and avoid myocardial depression.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For severe pulmonary hypertension, maintain SVR and preload or venous return, treat hypoxemia and hypercarbia, and avoid myocardial depression.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply an alternative supported aortic-stenosis plan.

## S02-Q023

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

A patient with atrial fibrillation and rheumatic mitral stenosis needs long-term stroke-prevention anticoagulation. Which management option best matches this scenario?

A. Use aspirin plus warfarin as the mechanical-heart-valve strategy.

B. Use warfarin and avoid a direct oral anticoagulant.

C. Select a direct oral anticoagulant for valvular atrial fibrillation.

D. Use risk-stratified bridging only when an existing warfarin regimen must be interrupted.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Use aspirin plus warfarin as the mechanical-heart-valve strategy.

Experts suggest combining ASA with warfarin in patients with mechanical heart valves, especially if the patient is at low risk of bleeding or at high thrombotic risk.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use aspirin plus warfarin as the mechanical-heart-valve strategy.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize warfarin use for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

**B — correct:** Use warfarin and avoid a direct oral anticoagulant.

Warfarin is used for prevention of stroke or systemic embolism in patients with atrial fibrillation, especially those with rheumatic mitral stenosis, where DOACs should be avoided.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize warfarin use for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

**C:** Select a direct oral anticoagulant for valvular atrial fibrillation.

Treatment with a DOAC is not recommended in patients with valvular atrial fibrillation, and these drugs are not approved for this use.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Select a direct oral anticoagulant for valvular atrial fibrillation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize warfarin use for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

**D:** Use risk-stratified bridging only when an existing warfarin regimen must be interrupted.

Bridging anticoagulation during warfarin interruption is suggested in patients at high risk for thromboembolism, not suggested in low-risk patients, and optional in intermediate-risk patients based on individual patient characteristics.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use risk-stratified bridging only when an existing warfarin regimen must be interrupted.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize warfarin use for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

## S02-Q024

Domain 2 · Arrhythmias and CIEDs

During surgery on a patient with an implanted cardioverter-defibrillator, a magnet is applied to address electrocautery interference. What response should be expected?

A. Magnet application induces an asynchronous mode in a majority of pacemakers.

B. Convert the ICD to an asynchronous pacemaker without affecting its antitachycardia function.

C. The magnet inhibits ICD tachyarrhythmia detection and therefore prevents shock delivery, without changing the underlying pacemaker mode.

D. Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Magnet application induces an asynchronous mode in a majority of pacemakers.

In a majority of pacemakers, magnet application induces an asynchronous mode.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Magnet application induces an asynchronous mode in a majority of pacemakers.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Predict ICD magnet effects on tachyarrhythmia therapy.

**B:** Convert the ICD to an asynchronous pacemaker without affecting its antitachycardia function.

Magnet application to ICDs may disable the antitachycardia function but not convert the device to an asynchronous pacemaker.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Convert the ICD to an asynchronous pacemaker without affecting its antitachycardia function.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Predict ICD magnet effects on tachyarrhythmia therapy.

**C — correct:** The magnet inhibits ICD tachyarrhythmia detection and therefore prevents shock delivery, without changing the underlying pacemaker mode.

Magnet application to an ICD inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection, thus preventing therapy (i.e., delivery of shocks), but it does not change the mode of the underlying pacemaker.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Predict ICD magnet effects on tachyarrhythmia therapy.

**D:** Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.

Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Predict ICD magnet effects on tachyarrhythmia therapy.

## S02-Q025

Domain 2 · Arrhythmias and CIEDs

Immediately before induction for elective surgery, a patient develops new-onset atrial fibrillation. Which perioperative plan is most appropriate if the operation can be deferred?

A. Use esmolol for ventricular-rate control and to increase conversion to sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.

B. Control the ventricular response rate as the immediate goal in new-onset atrial fibrillation.

C. Manage stable postoperative atrial fibrillation medically with rate or rhythm control and electrically cardiovert unstable patients.

D. Postpone surgery if possible until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Use esmolol for ventricular-rate control and to increase conversion to sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.

Esmolol has been shown to be equally effective in controlling the ventricular rate in patients with postoperative atrial fibrillation or flutter and in increasing the conversion rate to regular sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use esmolol for ventricular-rate control and to increase conversion to sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify preinduction atrial-fibrillation control.

**B:** Control the ventricular response rate as the immediate goal in new-onset atrial fibrillation.

Control of the ventricular response rate is the immediate goal in the treatment of new-onset atrial fibrillation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Control the ventricular response rate as the immediate goal in new-onset atrial fibrillation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify preinduction atrial-fibrillation control.

**C:** Manage stable postoperative atrial fibrillation medically with rate or rhythm control and electrically cardiovert unstable patients.

Patients with new-onset postoperative atrial fibrillation who are hemodynamically stable may be managed medically with rate or rhythm control, while hemodynamically unstable patients should be electrically cardioverted.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Manage stable postoperative atrial fibrillation medically with rate or rhythm control and electrically cardiovert unstable patients.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Identify preinduction atrial-fibrillation control.

**D — correct:** Postpone surgery if possible until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs prior to induction of anesthesia, surgery should be postponed, if possible, until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Identify preinduction atrial-fibrillation control.

According to Stoelting P&P, 6th edition, an adult cardiac-surgery team discusses whether an ACT threshold of 360 seconds can ever be accepted during cardiopulmonary bypass. Which interpretation matches the supplied evidence?

In pediatric cardiac surgery, the target ACT before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass is usually 480 seconds.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “In pediatric cardiac surgery, the target ACT before bypass is usually 480 seconds.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret an alternate bypass ACT threshold.

**D:** Most cardiac surgical centers target an ACT longer than 400 seconds during adult bypass.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Most cardiac surgical centers target an ACT longer than 400 seconds during adult bypass.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Interpret an alternate bypass ACT threshold.

## S02-Q027

Domain 2 · Cardiac surgery and CPB

After cardiopulmonary bypass, new ventricular dysfunction follows an episode in which air, thrombus, or calcium could have entered the coronary circulation. Which recognized ischemic etiology should be considered?

A. Kinked or clotted grafts are recognized technical ischemic causes of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

B. Coronary embolism from air, thrombus, or calcium can cause the post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

C. Inadequate myocardial protection is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

D. Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Kinked or clotted grafts are recognized technical ischemic causes of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

Technical difficulties such as kinked or clotted grafts are a recognized ischemic etiology of right or left ventricular dysfunction after cardiopulmonary bypass.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Kinked or clotted grafts are recognized technical ischemic causes of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize coronary embolism after bypass.

**B — correct:** Coronary embolism from air, thrombus, or calcium can cause the post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

Coronary embolism from air, thrombus, or calcium is a recognized ischemic etiology of right or left ventricular dysfunction after cardiopulmonary bypass.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize coronary embolism after bypass.

**C:** Inadequate myocardial protection is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

Inadequate myocardial protection is a recognized ischemic etiology of right or left ventricular dysfunction after cardiopulmonary bypass, distinct from graft-related technical problems or embolic events.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Inadequate myocardial protection is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize coronary embolism after bypass.

**D:** Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic etiology of right or left ventricular dysfunction after cardiopulmonary bypass.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize coronary embolism after bypass.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 3358, passage 249004

**D — correct:** Use moderate hypothermia to 30°–32°C, including spinal cooling, to reduce spinal-cord metabolism.

## S02-Q030

Domain 2 · Vascular aortic and carotid anesthesia

Which monitoring modality used during carotid endarterectomy is described as a poor indicator of cerebral-perfusion adequacy?

A. Carotid stump pressure.

B. Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.

C. Somatosensory evoked-potential monitoring.

D. Electroencephalography.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Carotid stump pressure.

Stump pressure (internal carotid artery back pressure) is a poor indicator of the adequacy of cerebral perfusion during carotid endarterectomy.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize stump-pressure limitation in carotid surgery.

**B:** Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.

Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography can be used to determine the need for shunt placement, to recognize shunt malfunction, and to manage postoperative hyperperfusion during carotid endarterectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize stump-pressure limitation in carotid surgery.

**C:** Somatosensory evoked-potential monitoring.

Somatosensory evoked potential monitoring can detect specific changes produced by decreased regional cerebral blood flow, but it can be difficult to determine whether these changes are due to anesthesia, hypothermia, changes in blood pressure, or cerebral ischemia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Somatosensory evoked-potential monitoring.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize stump-pressure limitation in carotid surgery.

**D:** Electroencephalography.

EEG may not detect subcortical or small cortical infarcts during carotid endarterectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Electroencephalography.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize stump-pressure limitation in carotid surgery.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 272, passage 233408

**D:** Gradual reduction of arterial blood pressure over several weeks before carotid endarterectomy restores intravascular volume, resets cerebral autoregulation to a more normal range, and improves perioperative management.

Gradual reduction of arterial blood pressure over several weeks before carotid endarterectomy restores intravascular volume, resets cerebral autoregulation to a more normal range, and improves perioperative management.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Gradual reduction of arterial blood pressure over several weeks before carotid endarterectomy restores intravascular volume, resets cerebral autoregulation to a more normal range, and improves perioperative management.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply induced hypertension during carotid clamping.

## S02-Q041

Domain 3 · Ethics consent and professionalism

An adolescent asks to discuss a sensitive health issue without a parent present. Before the issue and its potential risks have been assessed, which initial clinician action best supports trust?

A. When confidentiality entails minimal harm, encourage adolescents to be forthright with parents but respect their decision not to be.

B. When confidentiality may result in serious harm, clinicians may be ethically justified in notifying the parents.

C. Interview the adolescent in private and keep promises about confidentiality.

D. Provide emancipated and mature minors with a right to complete confidentiality.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** When confidentiality entails minimal harm, encourage adolescents to be forthright with parents but respect their decision not to be.

If maintaining confidentiality entails minimal harm, clinicians should encourage adolescents to be forthright with parents but respect their decision not to be. This is not the best initial action because the potential harm has not yet been assessed.

**B:** When confidentiality may result in serious harm, clinicians may be ethically justified in notifying the parents.

Parental notification may be ethically justified when maintaining confidentiality could result in serious harm. The stem supplies no evidence of serious harm, and that determination can only follow private assessment.

**C — correct:** Interview the adolescent in private and keep promises about confidentiality.

Interviewing the adolescent privately is the best initial action because it establishes trust and permits assessment of the concern and the applicable limits of confidentiality before parental involvement is considered.

**D:** Provide emancipated and mature minors with a right to complete confidentiality.

Emancipated and mature minors may have a right to complete confidentiality, but the stem does not establish either status. This legal principle does not replace the immediate step of conducting a private interview.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 126, passage 183040

## S02-Q043

Domain 3 · Statistics and evidence

In a randomized trial, groups receive different levels of care beyond the interventions being evaluated. Which bias does this describe?

A. Performance bias: systematic differences in care other than the preplanned interventions.

B. Selection bias in a randomized trial is systematic difference between patients receiving each intervention.

C. Attrition bias is systematic difference in withdrawal of patients from the two intervention groups.

D. Detection bias is systematic difference in ascertainment and recording of outcomes.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Performance bias: systematic differences in care other than the preplanned interventions.

Performance bias refers to systematic differences in care given to study patients other than the preplanned interventions being evaluated.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Distinguish performance bias from selection, attrition, and detection bias in a trial.

**B:** Selection bias in a randomized trial is systematic difference between patients receiving each intervention.

Selection bias in a randomized controlled trial refers to systematic differences between the patients receiving each intervention.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Selection bias in a randomized trial is systematic difference between patients receiving each intervention.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish performance bias from selection, attrition, and detection bias in a trial.

**C:** Attrition bias is systematic difference in withdrawal of patients from the two intervention groups.

Attrition bias refers to systematic differences in the withdrawal of patients from each of the two intervention groups.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Attrition bias is systematic difference in withdrawal of patients from the two intervention groups.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish performance bias from selection, attrition, and detection bias in a trial.

**D:** Detection bias is systematic difference in ascertainment and recording of outcomes.

Detection bias refers to systematic differences in the ascertainment and recording of outcomes.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Detection bias is systematic difference in ascertainment and recording of outcomes.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish performance bias from selection, attrition, and detection bias in a trial.

**Evidence sources:**

## S02-Q049

Domain 3 · Occupational environmental and infection hazards

Which pre-exposure measure specifically provides hepatitis B immunization and confirms that the healthcare provider developed an antibody response?

A. Wash hands frequently and thoroughly before and after patient care.

B. Dispose of used sharps immediately in puncture-proof receptacles and do not recap needles.

C. Provide the HBV vaccine series and post-vaccination antibody testing.

D. Use gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields as appropriate PPE.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Wash hands frequently and thoroughly before and after patient care.

Frequent, thorough hand hygiene is an important general infection-control measure, but it neither immunizes the provider against hepatitis B nor confirms an antibody response.

**B:** Dispose of used sharps immediately in puncture-proof receptacles and do not recap needles.

Immediate disposal of sharps without recapping reduces the risk of percutaneous exposure, but it does not establish or document hepatitis B immunity.

**C — correct:** Provide the HBV vaccine series and post-vaccination antibody testing.

Providing the hepatitis B vaccine series followed by post-vaccination antibody testing is the best answer because it provides pathogen-specific pre-exposure immunization and confirms that the provider developed a serologic response.

**D:** Use gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields as appropriate PPE.

Appropriate personal protective equipment provides barrier protection during anticipated exposure, but it does not immunize the provider or confirm hepatitis B immunity.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2010, passage 191850

## S02-Q059

Domain 4 · Multiorgan failure and ICU care

A patient has new confusion, a respiratory rate of 26 per minute, and a systolic blood pressure of 95 mm Hg. How should this qSOFA screen be interpreted?

A. Use serum creatinine above 0.8 mg/dL as a qSOFA criterion.

B. Require dysfunction in all six full-SOFA organ domains before identifying risk.

C. Use a mean arterial pressure below 70 mm Hg as the qSOFA blood-pressure criterion.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: D**

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use serum creatinine above 0.8 mg/dL as a qSOFA criterion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply qSOFA bedside criteria to identify patients at risk.

**B:** Require dysfunction in all six full-SOFA organ domains before identifying risk.

SOFA assesses neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function to quantify physiologic deterioration.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Require dysfunction in all six full-SOFA organ domains before identifying risk.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply qSOFA bedside criteria to identify patients at risk.

**C:** Use a mean arterial pressure below 70 mm Hg as the qSOFA blood-pressure criterion.

A mean arterial pressure below 70 mm Hg is considered abnormal in SOFA but may be normal in mid-pregnancy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use a mean arterial pressure below 70 mm Hg as the qSOFA blood-pressure criterion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply qSOFA bedside criteria to identify patients at risk.

**D — correct:** The patient is at risk because at least two qSOFA criteria are present.

Patients are at risk by qSOFA when two or more are present: confusion, respiratory rate above 22 per minute, or systolic blood pressure below 100 mm Hg.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply qSOFA bedside criteria to identify patients at risk.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 1357, passage 276292

**C:** Use REBOA as a temporary hemorrhage-control technique for hemodynamically significant pelvic fractures.

REBOA is a temporary hemorrhage-control technique for hemodynamically significant pelvic fractures.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use REBOA as a temporary hemorrhage-control technique for hemodynamically significant pelvic fractures.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select pelvic-binder use and positioning for suspected unstable pelvic-ring injury with hemorrhagic shock.

**D — correct:** Apply a pelvic binder or tightly knotted sheet.

## S02-Q105

According to Chestnut’s Obstetric Anesthesia, 6th edition, uterine atony is suspected immediately after delivery. While the oxytocin infusion continues and before second-line uterotonics are administered, which initial bedside maneuver is appropriate?

A. If oxytocin response is inadequate, employ additional uterotonic agents.

B. Use oxytocin as first-line prophylaxis for uterine atony after third-trimester delivery.

C. Use bimanual compression and uterine massage while continuing the oxytocin infusion to restore uterine tone.

**Answer: C**

**B:** Use oxytocin as first-line prophylaxis for uterine atony after third-trimester delivery.

Oxytocin is first-line prophylaxis after third-trimester delivery, but the patient now has suspected uterine atony and requires treatment rather than prophylaxis alone.

**C — correct:** Use bimanual compression and uterine massage while continuing the oxytocin infusion to restore uterine tone.

Bimanual compression and uterine massage while continuing oxytocin is the best answer because these are the initial bedside maneuvers used to restore uterine tone before escalation to additional uterotonics.

**D:** Recognize an atonic, poorly contractile uterus with vaginal bleeding as a common finding in uterine atony.

An atonic, poorly contractile uterus with vaginal bleeding helps identify uterine atony, but recognition of the finding is not a treatment maneuver.

Which hemodynamic profile describes burn shock?

A. A hyperdynamic, euvolemic state with increased cardiac output.

B. A hypodynamic, hypovolemic state with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output.

C. A distributive state with increased cardiac output and no volume loss.

D. Primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved intravascular volume.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: B**

**A:** A hyperdynamic, euvolemic state with increased cardiac output.

The source describes burn shock as hypodynamic and hypovolemic with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output, not a hyperdynamic euvolemic state.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A hyperdynamic, euvolemic state with increased cardiac output.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the early burn-shock hemodynamic profile of rapid intravascular fluid loss and reduced cardiac output.

**B — correct:** A hypodynamic, hypovolemic state with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output.

Burn shock is hypodynamic and hypovolemic, with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the early burn-shock hemodynamic profile of rapid intravascular fluid loss and reduced cardiac output.

**C:** A distributive state with increased cardiac output and no volume loss.

The source describes burn shock as hypovolemic with decreased cardiac output, not distributive shock with increased cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A distributive state with increased cardiac output and no volume loss.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the early burn-shock hemodynamic profile of rapid intravascular fluid loss and reduced cardiac output.

**D:** Primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved intravascular volume.

The source describes burn shock as rapid intravascular fluid loss, not primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved volume.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved intravascular volume.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the early burn-shock hemodynamic profile of rapid intravascular fluid loss and reduced cardiac output.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 3164, passage 295657
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 3164, passage 295658

## S02-Q136

Domain 9 · Common pediatric operations

An infant with pyloric stenosis has dehydration, hypochloremic hypokalemic alkalosis, and is not ready for induction. Which correction plan is most appropriate?

A. Proceed directly to induction because correction is immediate.

B. Replace free water alone because vomiting loses only water.

C. Promote renal hydrogen-ion excretion to accelerate correction.

D. Give normal saline with 20 mEq/L potassium at 10–20 mL/kg/h until volume, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate targets resolve.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Proceed directly to induction because correction is immediate.

In pyloric stenosis, electrolyte and fluid correction may take 24 to 48 hours depending on severity.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Proceed directly to induction because correction is immediate.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an anesthetic strategy for pyloric stenosis that corrects dehydration and alkalosis before induction.

**B:** Replace free water alone because vomiting loses only water.

Chronic vomiting results in large losses of hydrogen, chloride ions, and water, leading to an alkalotic dehydrated state.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Replace free water alone because vomiting loses only water.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an anesthetic strategy for pyloric stenosis that corrects dehydration and alkalosis before induction.

**C:** Promote renal hydrogen-ion excretion to accelerate correction.

Excretion of remaining hydrogen ions in exchange for sodium exacerbates or prevents resolution of the alkalosis.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Promote renal hydrogen-ion excretion to accelerate correction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an anesthetic strategy for pyloric stenosis that corrects dehydration and alkalosis before induction.

**D — correct:** Give normal saline with 20 mEq/L potassium at 10–20 mL/kg/h until volume, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate targets resolve.

In infants and children with chronic hypokalemic or hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis, correction is optimally achieved with NS containing 20 mEq/L potassium at 10 to 20 mL/kg per hour until potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, and hypovolemia targets are resolved.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select an anesthetic strategy for pyloric stenosis that corrects dehydration and alkalosis before induction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 396, passage 184369
- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 396, passage 184368

## S02-Q137

Domain 9 · Fluids blood glucose and temperature

During prolonged neonatal anesthesia, approximately what endogenous glucose-production rate must gluconeogenesis provide?

A. Approximately 4 mg/kg/min.

B. A rate equal to 60%–70% of the maternal serum glucose concentration.

C. A rate defined by the onset of jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, or seizures.

D. A D10W bolus of 2–4 mL/kg followed by no glucose infusion.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Approximately 4 mg/kg/min.

Gluconeogenesis in a neonate must proceed at approximately 4 mg/kg per minute.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply neonatal glucose utilization and replacement principles during prolonged anesthesia.

**B:** A rate equal to 60%–70% of the maternal serum glucose concentration.

At birth, fetal serum glucose concentration is 60% to 70% of the maternal value.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A rate equal to 60%–70% of the maternal serum glucose concentration.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply neonatal glucose utilization and replacement principles during prolonged anesthesia.

**C:** A rate defined by the onset of jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, or seizures.

Symptoms of neonatal hypoglycemia may include jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, and seizures.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A rate defined by the onset of jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, or seizures.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply neonatal glucose utilization and replacement principles during prolonged anesthesia.

**D:** A D10W bolus of 2–4 mL/kg followed by no glucose infusion.

D10W may be given as a 2 to 4 mL/kg bolus followed by an infusion of 4 to 6 mg/kg per minute.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A D10W bolus of 2–4 mL/kg followed by no glucose infusion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply neonatal glucose utilization and replacement principles during prolonged anesthesia.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 384, passage 184304

## S02-Q172

C. A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis is consistent with acute respiratory failure.

**B:** In acute respiratory failure, PaCO2 can be increased, unchanged, or decreased depending on alveolar ventilation relative to metabolic carbon-dioxide production.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “In acute respiratory failure, PaCO2 can be increased, unchanged, or decreased depending on alveolar ventilation relative to metabolic carbon-dioxide production.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate acid-base compensation, PaCO2, and alveolar ventilation to distinguish acute from chronic respiratory failure.

**C:** A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis is consistent with acute respiratory failure.

A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis is consistent with acute respiratory failure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis is consistent with acute respiratory failure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate acid-base compensation, PaCO2, and alveolar ventilation to distinguish acute from chronic respiratory failure.

**D — correct:** Acute respiratory failure typically causes an abrupt PaCO2 increase with a corresponding pH decrease, whereas chronic respiratory failure has near-normal pH despite increased PaCO2 because of renal bicarbonate reabsorption.

Acute respiratory failure typically causes an abrupt PaCO2 increase with a corresponding pH decrease, whereas chronic respiratory failure has near-normal pH despite increased PaCO2 because of renal bicarbonate reabsorption.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate acid-base compensation, PaCO2, and alveolar ventilation to distinguish acute from chronic respiratory failure.

## S02-Q173

Domain 10 · Perioperative systems and enhanced recovery

Compared with systemic opioids, which perioperative regional-analgesia relationship is supported?

A. Neuraxial and peripheral regional analgesia can provide superior analgesia and improve outcomes including major morbidity.

B. Use multimodal agents acting at different mechanisms and nervous-system sites.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use multimodal agents acting at different mechanisms and nervous-system sites.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the analgesic and outcome benefits of perioperative neuraxial or peripheral regional analgesia compared with systemic opioids.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Extend pain interventions into the postoperative phase for maximum benefit.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the analgesic and outcome benefits of perioperative neuraxial or peripheral regional analgesia compared with systemic opioids.

**D:** Use aggressive nonopioid multimodal interventions throughout the perioperative period.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use aggressive nonopioid multimodal interventions throughout the perioperative period.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the analgesic and outcome benefits of perioperative neuraxial or peripheral regional analgesia compared with systemic opioids.

## S02-Q186

Domain 11 · Neuromuscular blockers and reversal

In severe renal and hepatic dysfunction, which nondepolarizing-blocker profile provides organ-independent elimination?

A. Vecuronium is partly renally eliminated and lasts longer in renal failure.

B. Approximately 40%–50% of pancuronium is excreted in urine.

C. Atracurium and cisatracurium undergo organ-independent Hofmann elimination.

D. Cisatracurium is four times as potent as atracurium and does not release histamine.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Vecuronium is partly renally eliminated and lasts longer in renal failure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize cisatracurium and atracurium organ-independent Hofmann elimination when renal or hepatic clearance is impaired.

**B:** Approximately 40%–50% of pancuronium is excreted in urine.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Approximately 40%–50% of pancuronium is excreted in urine.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize cisatracurium and atracurium organ-independent Hofmann elimination when renal or hepatic clearance is impaired.

**C — correct:** Atracurium and cisatracurium undergo organ-independent Hofmann elimination.

Atracurium undergoes ester hydrolysis and nonenzymatic alkaline Hofmann elimination to inactive products that do not depend on renal excretion; cisatracurium is predominantly cleared by organ-independent Hofmann elimination.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize cisatracurium and atracurium organ-independent Hofmann elimination when renal or hepatic clearance is impaired.

**D:** Cisatracurium is four times as potent as atracurium and does not release histamine.

Cisatracurium is approximately four times as potent as atracurium and does not cause histamine release.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Cisatracurium is four times as potent as atracurium and does not release histamine.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize cisatracurium and atracurium organ-independent Hofmann elimination when renal or hepatic clearance is impaired.

## S02-Q187

Domain 11 · Neuromuscular blockers and reversal

After nondepolarizing neuromuscular blockade, which monitoring and reversal plan best establishes readiness for tracheal extubation?

A. At a train-of-four count of 2 or more, give sugammadex 2 mg/kg and extubate after 2 to 4 minutes without confirming the ratio.

B. Use head lift and handgrip as sufficient evidence that clinically important residual paralysis is absent.

C. If no tactile or visual train-of-four fade is seen, assume the train-of-four ratio is adequate for extubation.

D. Use quantitative train-of-four monitoring, select reversal according to block depth, and confirm a train-of-four ratio of at least 0.9 before extubation.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**B:** Use head lift and handgrip as sufficient evidence that clinically important residual paralysis is absent.

Clinical assessments such as head lift and handgrip are insensitive for identifying residual paralysis.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use head lift and handgrip as sufficient evidence that clinically important residual paralysis is absent.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select neuromuscular-blocker monitoring and reversal timing by integrating train-of-four recovery with extubation readiness.

**C:** If no tactile or visual train-of-four fade is seen, assume the train-of-four ratio is adequate for extubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “If no tactile or visual train-of-four fade is seen, assume the train-of-four ratio is adequate for extubation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select neuromuscular-blocker monitoring and reversal timing by integrating train-of-four recovery with extubation readiness.

**D — correct:** Use quantitative train-of-four monitoring, select reversal according to block depth, and confirm a train-of-four ratio of at least 0.9 before extubation.

Use quantitative train-of-four monitoring, select reversal according to block depth, and confirm a train-of-four ratio of at least 0.9 before tracheal extubation.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select neuromuscular-blocker monitoring and reversal timing by integrating train-of-four recovery with extubation readiness.

## S02-Q189

Domain 11 · Local anesthetics and LAST

Which statement addresses both ester-versus-amide metabolism and their allergy distinction?

A. Amide agents have largely supplanted ester agents in modern anesthesia.

B. Esters use plasma cholinesterase and often form allergenic PABA; common amides use hepatic CYP metabolism and true amide allergy is rare.

C. Local anesthetics block sodium channels.

D. CYP1A2 and CYP3A4 maturation affects amide toxicity risk in young infants.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Amide agents have largely supplanted ester agents in modern anesthesia.

Ester anesthetics have been supplanted by amide local anesthetics in modern anesthesia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Amide agents have largely supplanted ester agents in modern anesthesia.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish ester from amide local-anesthetic metabolism and relate the distinction to hepatic function and allergy history.

**B — correct:** Esters use plasma cholinesterase and often form allergenic PABA; common amides use hepatic CYP metabolism and true amide allergy is rare.

Ester local anesthetics are metabolized by plasma butyrylcholinesterase, whereas common amide agents are metabolized hepatically by CYP enzymes such as CYP3A4 or CYP1A2; ester metabolism to para-aminobenzoic acid is a usual allergy mechanism, while true amide allergy is rare.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Distinguish ester from amide local-anesthetic metabolism and relate the distinction to hepatic function and allergy history.

**C:** Local anesthetics block sodium channels.

Local anesthetics are sodium-channel blockers.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Local anesthetics block sodium channels.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish ester from amide local-anesthetic metabolism and relate the distinction to hepatic function and allergy history.

**D:** CYP1A2 and CYP3A4 maturation affects amide toxicity risk in young infants.

CYP1A2 is not fully mature before age 3 years and CYP3A4 is immature at birth, increasing amide toxicity risk in infants younger than 6 months.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “CYP1A2 and CYP3A4 maturation affects amide toxicity risk in young infants.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish ester from amide local-anesthetic metabolism and relate the distinction to hepatic function and allergy history.

## S02-Q205

Neuraxial hematoma is reported more commonly after which block type?

A. Epidural block rather than subarachnoid block.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Only lesions already causing spinal ischemia or infarction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize that neuraxial hematoma is more common after epidural than subarachnoid block.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Only neuraxial blocks addressed by ASRA antithrombotic guidance.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize that neuraxial hematoma is more common after epidural than subarachnoid block.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Select local infiltration, peripheral nerve blocks, and neuraxial analgesia as the local-anesthetic techniques in a multimodal pathway.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate regional, nonopioid, and opioid-sparing strategies with functional recovery goals after major surgery.

**D:** Use spinally delivered substances because they can reverse or block antinociceptive tolerance and opioid-induced hyperalgesia.

Spinally delivered substances can reverse or block antinociceptive tolerance and opioid-induced hyperalgesia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use spinally delivered substances because they can reverse or block antinociceptive tolerance and opioid-induced hyperalgesia.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize opioid-induced hyperalgesia as paradoxical pain after chronic opioid exposure and distinguish it from tolerance.

## S02-Q211

**D:** Use ketamine because NMDA antagonism reduces morphine tolerance in rodents.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use ketamine because NMDA antagonism reduces morphine tolerance in rodents.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate opioid-induced hyperalgesia, tolerance, and spinally delivered analgesic strategies in a patient with chronic pain.

## S02-Q223

Domain 13 · Lung isolation and OLV

During one-lung ventilation, which physiologic response helps preserve ventilation-perfusion matching?

A. Pulmonary vascular resistance is lowest at FRC and rises when lung volume moves above or below FRC.

B. Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction improves ventilation-perfusion matching, although anesthetic interventions can impair it.

C. Keep the ventilated lung near FRC while facilitating collapse of the nonventilated lung to optimize redistribution.

D. Maximize pulmonary vascular resistance in the ventilated lung and minimize it in the nonventilated lung.

**B — correct:** Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction improves ventilation-perfusion matching, although anesthetic interventions can impair it.

During OLV, hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction improves V/Q matching, although anesthetic interventions can impair this response.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the physiologic basis and expected response to hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation when pulmonary blood flow remains directed toward the nonventilated lung.

**C:** Keep the ventilated lung near FRC while facilitating collapse of the nonventilated lung to optimize redistribution.

Maintaining the ventilated lung near FRC while facilitating collapse of the nonventilated lung optimizes pulmonary-flow redistribution.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Keep the ventilated lung near FRC while facilitating collapse of the nonventilated lung to optimize redistribution.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the physiologic basis and expected response to hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation when pulmonary blood flow remains directed toward the nonventilated lung.

**D:** Maximize pulmonary vascular resistance in the ventilated lung and minimize it in the nonventilated lung.

The anesthesiologist’s OLV goal is to maximize PVR in the nonventilated lung while minimizing PVR in the ventilated lung.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Maximize pulmonary vascular resistance in the ventilated lung and minimize it in the nonventilated lung.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the physiologic basis and expected response to hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation when pulmonary blood flow remains directed toward the nonventilated lung.

## S02-Q224

Domain 13 · Lung isolation and OLV

A patient’s right-upper-lobe bronchus originates close to the carina, making right-sided double-lumen placement vulnerable to obstruction. Which tube choice is most appropriate?

A. Choose a right-sided DLT with a modified cuff or slot that ventilates the right upper lobe.

B. Choose a right-sided DLT when its slot aligns with the right-upper-lobe bronchus and distal bronchi remain visible.

C. Choose a left-sided double-lumen tube because the short right mainstem and upper-lobe anatomy leave a greater safety margin on the left.

D. Choose a right-sided DLT when the left mainstem is distorted or for left lung transplantation or resection.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Choose a right-sided DLT with a modified cuff or slot that ventilates the right upper lobe.

A right-sided DLT incorporates a modified cuff or slot that allows ventilation of the right upper lobe.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Choose a right-sided DLT with a modified cuff or slot that ventilates the right upper lobe.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select a left-sided double-lumen tube when right-upper-lobe bronchial anatomy makes right-sided tube placement less forgiving.

**B:** Choose a right-sided DLT when its slot aligns with the right-upper-lobe bronchus and distal bronchi remain visible.

A right-sided DLT can be effective when its opening slot aligns with the right-upper-lobe bronchus and the bronchus intermedius and lower-lobe bronchus remain visible.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Choose a right-sided DLT when its slot aligns with the right-upper-lobe bronchus and distal bronchi remain visible.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select a left-sided double-lumen tube when right-upper-lobe bronchial anatomy makes right-sided tube placement less forgiving.

**C — correct:** Choose a left-sided double-lumen tube because the short right mainstem and upper-lobe anatomy leave a greater safety margin on the left.

Choose a left-sided DLT because the short right mainstem and right-upper-lobe origin close to the carina make right-sided intubation prone to right-upper-lobe obstruction, while the left-sided tube has a greater safety margin.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select a left-sided double-lumen tube when right-upper-lobe bronchial anatomy makes right-sided tube placement less forgiving.

**D:** Choose a right-sided DLT when the left mainstem is distorted or for left lung transplantation or resection.

A right-sided DLT is indicated when the left mainstem entrance is distorted or compressed, or for left lung transplantation, left-sided sleeve resection, or left pneumonectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Choose a right-sided DLT when the left mainstem is distorted or for left lung transplantation or resection.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select a left-sided double-lumen tube when right-upper-lobe bronchial anatomy makes right-sided tube placement less forgiving.

## S02-Q225

Domain 13 · Thoracic procedures

After lung resection with one-lung ventilation, which perioperative strategy best reduces acute lung-injury risk in the reduced residual pulmonary vascular bed?

A. Treat right pneumonectomy as lower risk than left because left-lung lymphatic drainage is more compromised.

B. Avoid restrictive fluid management because it has been shown to increase oliguria and acute kidney injury after thoracic surgery.

C. Accept more than 2 L perioperative fluid and 10 mL/kg OLV tidal volumes to prevent postpneumonectomy edema.

D. Use protective OLV and avoid excess perioperative fluid because fluid excess independently increases acute lung-injury risk while the reduced vascular bed increases endothelial stress.

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Treat right pneumonectomy as lower risk than left because left-lung lymphatic drainage is more compromised.

Right pneumonectomy has higher pulmonary-edema risk than left pneumonectomy, in part because more than half of remaining-left-lung lymphatic drainage is compromised.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Treat right pneumonectomy as lower risk than left because left-lung lymphatic drainage is more compromised.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate one-lung ventilation, pulmonary vascular load, and fluid restriction to reduce the risk of acute lung injury after lung resection.

**B:** Avoid restrictive fluid management because it has been shown to increase oliguria and acute kidney injury after thoracic surgery.

Restrictive fluid management after thoracic surgery has not been shown to increase oliguria or acute kidney injury in pooled studies.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Avoid restrictive fluid management because it has been shown to increase oliguria and acute kidney injury after thoracic surgery.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate one-lung ventilation, pulmonary vascular load, and fluid restriction to reduce the risk of acute lung injury after lung resection.

**C:** Accept more than 2 L perioperative fluid and 10 mL/kg OLV tidal volumes to prevent postpneumonectomy edema.

Postpneumonectomy pulmonary edema is associated with perioperative fluid overload exceeding 2 L and higher OLV tidal volumes of 10 mL/kg.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Accept more than 2 L perioperative fluid and 10 mL/kg OLV tidal volumes to prevent postpneumonectomy edema.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate one-lung ventilation, pulmonary vascular load, and fluid restriction to reduce the risk of acute lung injury after lung resection.

**D — correct:** Use protective OLV and avoid excess perioperative fluid because fluid excess independently increases acute lung-injury risk while the reduced vascular bed increases endothelial stress.

After lung resection, avoid excess perioperative fluid and use protective OLV because excess fluid independently increases early acute lung-injury risk while reduced residual pulmonary vascular bed increases endothelial stress.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate one-lung ventilation, pulmonary vascular load, and fluid restriction to reduce the risk of acute lung injury after lung resection.

## S02-Q226

Domain 13 · Thoracic procedures

During open pneumonectomy, the pulmonary vascular bed is abruptly reduced and pulmonary-artery clamping produces hemodynamic deterioration. Which consequence should be anticipated?

A. The right ventricle must pump normal cardiac output through a reduced vascular bed, and clamping can cause acute right-heart overload, dilation, ischemia, or arrhythmia.

B. High PEEP increases right-ventricular impedance and can cause dilation and decreased contractility.

C. Expect cardiac herniation as the common early complication within 24 hours of intrapericardial pneumonectomy.

D. Use a temporary slow pulmonary-artery clamp test to assess the right-heart response.

**Answer: A**

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the hemodynamic and ventilatory consequences of open pneumonectomy and adjust anesthetic management for the reduced pulmonary vascular bed.

**B:** High PEEP increases right-ventricular impedance and can cause dilation and decreased contractility.

**C:** Expect cardiac herniation as the common early complication within 24 hours of intrapericardial pneumonectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Expect cardiac herniation as the common early complication within 24 hours of intrapericardial pneumonectomy.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the hemodynamic and ventilatory consequences of open pneumonectomy and adjust anesthetic management for the reduced pulmonary vascular bed.

**D:** Use a temporary slow pulmonary-artery clamp test to assess the right-heart response.

A temporary slow pulmonary-artery clamp test can evaluate the clinical and echocardiographic response of the right heart.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use a temporary slow pulmonary-artery clamp test to assess the right-heart response.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the hemodynamic and ventilatory consequences of open pneumonectomy and adjust anesthetic management for the reduced pulmonary vascular bed.

## S02-Q228

Domain 13 · Postoperative pulmonary complications

A patient develops postoperative hypoxemia after pulmonary-resection thoracotomy with shallow breathing and poor secretion clearance. Which initial management strategy is most appropriate before invasive ventilation?

A. Assume atelectasis is inevitable because it has been reported in up to 100% after pulmonary-resection thoracotomy.

B. Attribute the problem to poor cough and secretion clearance without adding lung-expansion measures.

C. Treat pain splinting and rapid shallow breathing with physiotherapy, mobilization, secretion clearance, and early CPAP when appropriate before intubation.

D. Increase FiO2 rather than use physiotherapy because hypoxemic patients benefit more from oxygen concentration alone.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**Answer: C**

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Assume atelectasis is inevitable because it has been reported in up to 100% after pulmonary-resection thoracotomy.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize postoperative atelectasis from shallow breathing and choose lung-expansion measures before escalation to invasive ventilation.

**B:** Attribute the problem to poor cough and secretion clearance without adding lung-expansion measures.

A poor cough and limited secretion clearance add to postoperative atelectasis.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Attribute the problem to poor cough and secretion clearance without adding lung-expansion measures.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize postoperative atelectasis from shallow breathing and choose lung-expansion measures before escalation to invasive ventilation.

**C — correct:** Treat pain splinting and rapid shallow breathing with physiotherapy, mobilization, secretion clearance, and early CPAP when appropriate before intubation.

Pain splinting and rapid shallow breathing promote small-airway closure and alveolar collapse; use physiotherapy, early mobilization, secretion clearance, and early CPAP for hypoxemia before escalating to intubation when clinically appropriate.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize postoperative atelectasis from shallow breathing and choose lung-expansion measures before escalation to invasive ventilation.

**D:** Increase FiO2 rather than use physiotherapy because hypoxemic patients benefit more from oxygen concentration alone.

Many hypoxemic patients benefit more from physiotherapy to increase FRC and PaO2 than from further increasing FiO2.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increase FiO2 rather than use physiotherapy because hypoxemic patients benefit more from oxygen concentration alone.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize postoperative atelectasis from shallow breathing and choose lung-expansion measures before escalation to invasive ventilation.

## S02-Q229

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Deprioritize PE because it occurred in only 5% of thoracotomy patients before heparin prophylaxis.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate timing, pleuritic symptoms, oxygenation, and hemodynamics to recognize postoperative pulmonary embolism among causes of respiratory deterioration.

**D — correct:** Postoperative pulmonary embolism, given the timing, pleuritic symptoms, oxygenation/hemodynamic deterioration, and malignancy or major-surgery risk.

## S02-Q230

Early after lung transplantation, a patient develops pulmonary edema; later surveillance shows a declining FEV1/FVC ratio. Which interpretation best distinguishes graft injury from later graft or airway complications?

C. Prefer a left-sided DLT after transplantation because the right upper-lobe bronchus makes right-sided positioning difficult.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Diagnose primary graft dysfunction solely from the fact that severe cases can be managed with VV-ECMO.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize primary graft dysfunction after lung transplantation and distinguish early diffuse graft injury from airway anastomotic complications.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Prefer a left-sided DLT after transplantation because the right upper-lobe bronchus makes right-sided positioning difficult.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize primary graft dysfunction after lung transplantation and distinguish early diffuse graft injury from airway anastomotic complications.

**D:** Attribute unequal native and transplanted lung compliance to overdistension, mediastinal shift, barotrauma, and tension pneumothoraces.

Unequal native and transplanted lung compliance can cause overdistension, mediastinal shift, barotrauma, and tension pneumothoraces.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Attribute unequal native and transplanted lung compliance to overdistension, mediastinal shift, barotrauma, and tension pneumothoraces.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize primary graft dysfunction after lung transplantation and distinguish early diffuse graft injury from airway anastomotic complications.

**D:** Recognize that increased intraabdominal pressure during pregnancy can reduce maternal cardiac output and uteroplacental perfusion.
